Re: 
Case 19-G-0309 – Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, 
Charges, Rules and Regulations of the Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a 
National Grid NY for Gas Service  
 
 
Case 19-G-0310 – Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, 
Charges, Rules and Regulations of KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National 
Grid for Gas Service 
 
Dear Secretary Burgess: 
 
Due to the unprecedented demands on City of New York (“City”) personnel and resources 
responding to the COVID-19 pandemic, the City is unable to provide an Initial Post-Hearing Brief 
in Cases 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310. The City submits this letter asking that the Public Service 
Commission (“Commission”) take its prefiled testimony on all issues into consideration in making 
its decisions in these cases and reiterates the testimony supporting several of those positions below. 
 
Policy Considerations 
 
Even before the economic devastation that the COVID-19 pandemic has brought to the  
City, which will continue to be felt, and even multiplied, in coming months and years, the City 
urged that the Commission would have to balance a number of important policies in deciding these 
cases. As set forth in the testimony of the City’s Policy Panel, affordable energy prices are critical 
to economic development and the continued prosperity of New York City, and in turn, New York 
State.  (Tr. 5496.)  As noted below, Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY 
(“KEDNY”) and KeySpan East Gas Corp. d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI,” and collectively with 
KEDNY, “National Grid” or “the Companies”) are seeking extremely large rate increases that, if 
granted, will impose very significant burdens on all KEDNY and KEDLI customers. In addition, 
the Companies are seeking surcharges and shareholder rewards in excess of $125 million beyond 
their requested increases in rates.  (Tr. 5502.)  Given the incredible financial burden of the 
pandemic, the impact of these bill increases on ratepayers will be magnified. 
  


--- Page 2 ---
April 6, 2020 
Page 2 
 
 
In addition, the City’s low income residents already are over-burdened with energy bills. 
Fundamentally, in deciding these cases, the Commission must ensure that ratepayers are not 
burdened with unfair bill increases during these historically difficult times, and it should provide 
additional protections for the City’s most vulnerable residents.  
  
 
The Commission also must ensure that important climate change policies are pursued. The 
City and State have enacted laws and policies to achieve expeditious and equitable decarbonization 
and sustainability.  For example, the City has made unprecedented commitments to achieve its 
objectives of creating a resilient and low-carbon energy supply, improving air quality, and 
achieving carbon neutrality by 2050, as set forth in OneNYC 2050: Building a Strong and Fair 
City (“OneNYC”).   The City’s laws and policies align with the State’s robust array of climate 
policies, and achievement of the City’s objectives would help achieve the State’s objectives, and 
vice versa.  During the pendency of these cases, New York enacted the sweeping Climate 
Leadership and Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”), which sets the objective of 70 percent 
renewable electricity production by 2030 and zero-emission electricity generation by 2040.  
 
In order to ensure that the goals of the aforementioned City and State laws and policies are 
achieved, the Commission must require that the Companies pursue immediate and aggressive 
increases in energy efficiency and demand response programs as an alternative to traditional 
infrastructure investments.  It is the Commission’s challenge in this proceeding to ensure that the 
Companies can provide safe and reliable service at just and reasonable rates, and at the same time 
implement important City and State climate goals. 
 
Low Income  
 
The Commission should eliminate reconnection fees altogether for both KEDNY and 
KEDLI low income customers.  As the NYC Low Income Panel (“LIP”) testified: 
 
Q. 
WHY SHOULD KEDNY ELIMINATE ITS RECONNECTION FEES 
FOR LOW INCOME CUSTOMERS?  
 
A. 
Eliminating reconnection fees for low income customers is appropriate 
because it will provide them with a better opportunity to remain utility 
customers and pay their utility bills.  In particular, low income customers 
who are facing termination of service already have demonstrated an 
inability to pay utility bills.  Requiring these customers to pay a fee to 
reconnect their gas service detracts from the amount they can put toward 
paying their past due bills, and creates an additional financial barrier for 
them to overcome.  Customers and the utilities would be much better served 
if this money was instead put towards direct payment of the customers’ 
utility bills.  
 
 


--- Page 3 ---
April 6, 2020 
Page 3 
 
 
Moreover, the amount collected for reconnections is very small when 
considered in context.  According to National Grid’s response to City IR-
106, on average over the last five years, KEDNY collected approximately 
$1.3 million in reconnection fees annually from all customers.  According 
to National Grid’s response to DPS IR-260, approximately $174,000 of that 
amount was collected annually from low income customers over the same 
time period.  These amounts, and in particular the amount collected from 
low income customers, are insignificant when considered in context of the 
revenue requirement increase that KEDNY is seeking in this rate case, 
approximately $196 million in the Rate Year.  We also understand that each 
Company’s actual annual gas revenue requirements are in the billions of 
dollars.  
  
 
Conversely, KEDNY’s reconnection fees are substantial, particularly from 
the perspective of a low income customer.  As previously noted, KEDNY 
charges a reconnection fee of $66.13 (plus fees) for reconnecting service at 
the meter, which represents nearly 58 percent of an average KEDNY low 
income heating customer’s monthly gas bill.  Thus, in context, the loss of 
these reconnection revenues would be inconsequential for the Companies, 
but the savings could be material for a customer who already cannot afford 
his or her utility bill. 
 
 
Finally, elimination of KEDNY’s reconnection fees for low income 
customers is appropriate to harmonize the utility’s practices with those 
already adopted by KEDLI.  This eliminates the possibility of low income 
customers residing in different portions of New York City (for example, on 
the Rockaways versus in Brooklyn) being subject to different rules on 
reconnection fees.  (Tr. 5369-5371.) 
 
Gas Safety 
 
 
Ensuring the safety of New York City’s gas system is of utmost importance. The 
Commission should continue pre-existing gas safety metrics as part of the forthcoming one year 
rate plan, subject to reasonable tightening of the individual metrics to ensure performance 
continues to improve over time.  In addition, the Commission should not modify the current 
allocation of the 150 at-risk basis points among the safety metrics.   
 
Attached to this letter is Exhibit 547, which provides the City’s proposed incentive 
mechanisms and associated basis point allocations, including a comparison of the City’s proposal 
against National Grid’s current incentive mechanisms and basis point allocations. 
 


--- Page 4 ---
April 6, 2020 
Page 4 
 
 
Specific to the Leak Backlog Metric, the Commission should modify the targets such that 
the leak backlogs for both KEDNY and KEDLI are fully eliminated in five years.  The City 
reiterates its position that these modified leak backlog targets can be achieved using the 
Companies’ proposed funding amounts.  As the NYC Gas Infrastructure and Safety Panel 
(“GISP”) testified: 
 
Q. 
DO YOU BELIEVE THAT IT IS POSSIBLE FOR NATIONAL GRID TO 
ACHIEVE YOUR PROPOSED LEAK BACKLOG THRESHOLDS 
USING PROPOSED FUNDING AMOUNTS SET FORTH IN THE 
COMPANIES’ PRE-FILED DIRECT TESTIMONY? 
 
A. 
Yes.  Both Companies have made significant reductions in their total leak 
backlogs since 2014 – KEDNY has eliminated on average 316 leaks per 
year, and KEDLI has eliminated on average of 830, with both showing 
greater reductions in recent years as Proactive LPP replacement programs 
continue to ramp up.  Combined with the synergies from replacing LPP, we 
believe that both Companies are capable of meeting these targets 
(particularly if, as proposed, KEDNY’s Proactive LPP replacement 
program continues to ramp up over the next several years).   
 
 
Moreover, we are proposing to reset the leak backlog targets to the actual 
leaks remaining at the end of CY 2018 (2,803 for KEDNY; 8,199 for 
KEDLI).  For KEDNY, at least, the Company should already be well below 
this target by the time the Rate Year starts.  KEDNY reported in response 
to CNY-38 that its total leak backlog as of June 11, 2019 was 2,467, which 
is 336 leaks below our proposed reset of 2,803.  KEDNY has another seven 
months before the Rate Year commences to lower its actual backlog even 
further, which should make these proposed annual reductions that much 
easier to attain.  
 
 
We understand that the Company identifies and repairs hundreds of leaks 
of all types each year.  However, it has been doing so for many years, and 
it still has been able to eliminate a significant portion of its leak backlog as 
well.  It may take five years to fully eliminate the backlog under our 
proposal, but that elimination should be the goal.  Put another way, the goal 
should not be to simply maintain thousands of minor leaks each year (i.e., 
the status quo).  (Tr. 5473-5474.) 
 
Moreover, as the NYC GISP testified, the Commission should modify National Grid’s leak 
survey practices: 
 
Q. 
WHAT IS YOUR RECOMMENDATION? 


--- Page 5 ---
April 6, 2020 
Page 5 
 
 
 
A. 
National Grid’s leak survey practices should be aligned both internally and 
with Con Edison, so that the entire City is assessed in a consistent fashion.  
For example, KEDNY should conduct monthly mobile ‘low speed’ leak 
surveys, and KEDLI should perform similar surveys in the Rockaways 
portion of its service territory.  In addition, KEDNY should perform 
walking surveys of gas service connections in non-business districts every 
three years, to align its practices with Con Edison and KEDLI.  (Tr. 5478.) 
 
Revenue Allocation and Rate Design 
 
 
National Grid’s proposed interclass revenue allocation should be adopted at whatever 
revenue requirements the Commission sets for the Companies.  (Ex. 727, attached hereto.)  As the 
City Rate Design Panel (“RDP”) testified, the cost of service studies that the Companies filed are 
consistent with prior practice and Commission precedent and properly allocate the customer 
portion of distribution mains based on the number of customers and the demand component based 
on class contribution to design-day demand. (Tr. 5543-5544.)  The City’s RDP, including expert 
witness Mr. Richard Baudino, testified that the Companies’ embedded cost of service studies 
(“ECOSS”), which utilized the minimum-system methodology to allocate distribution main costs, 
were consistent with accepted cost-causation principles and prior Commission precedent: 
 
Q. 
HAS THE COMMISSION RECOGNIZED THE APPROPRIATENESS 
OF A CUSTOMER COMPONENT IN THE COST OF GAS 
DISTRIBUTION MAINS? 
 
A. 
Yes.  The Commission has accepted the use of a minimum-system 
methodology to classify and allocate distribution costs in past National Grid 
cases.   
 
Q. 
DO YOU AGREE WITH THE COMPANIES’ CLASSIFICATION AND 
ALLOCATION OF DISTRIBUTION MAINS? 
 
A. 
Yes.  As the RDP pointed out, distribution mains are installed to connect 
customers to the distribution system and to provide capacity to meet the 
winter peak.  The Companies’ ECOSS appropriately utilized a minimum-
system study to estimate which portions of distribution mains are demand 
related and customer related, and then allocated costs to the various 
customer classes based on that relationship.  We recommend that the 
Commission adopt the Companies’ minimum-system methodology for 
classifying and allocating the cost of distribution mains.  (Tr. 5544.)  
 


--- Page 6 ---
April 6, 2020 
Page 6 
 
 
With respect to KEDNY’s proposed non-firm rates, all parties agreed that it is critical that 
National Grid retain all current non-firm customers and continue to convert new ones in order to 
meet projected demand into 2035.  (Tr. 2961-2962).1  Therefore, National Grid’s proposal to 
reduce the rates for non-firm service should be approved.2 For KEDNY, although the large subsidy 
being provided by non-firm customers would support more than a 2.4 percent decrease that is 
included in KEDNY’s proposed interclass revenue allocation, the City supports the Tier 1 and Tier 
2 rates proposed by the Companies.  (Tr. 2830-2831.)  The City RDP testified: 
 
For purposes of this case, we support the RDP’s proposed rates and revenue 
allocation for the new SC 18-22 and SC-22 TC/IT classes, although TC/IT 
customers are providing a very large subsidy to other service classes and 
could have their rates reduced more significantly based on the results of the 
Companies’ ECOSS.  (Tr. 5547.) 
 
For KEDLI, however, the City RDP took exception to the proposed increase to the non-firm rate 
classes that are providing a huge subsidy to the other classes. On rebuttal, the KEDLI Rate Design 
Panel agreed with the City and proposed a zero increase for the non-firm rate classes.  (Tr. 2825.) 
 
 
Importantly, with respect to non-firm rates, the City RDP emphasized that the proposed 
non-firm rates should be adjusted downward if the revenue requirements awarded by the 
Commission are less than what the Companies seek:  
 
Q. 
IF 
THE 
COMMISSION 
APPROVES 
A 
LOWER 
REVENUE 
REQUIREMENT INCREASE THAN KEDNY OR KEDLI ARE 
SEEKING IN THIS CASE, HOW SHOULD THIS LOWER INCREASE 
BE SPREAD AMONG THE COMPANIES’ SERVICE CLASSES? 
 
 
1  C.f. Case 19-G-0678 – Proceeding on Motion of the Commission to Investigate Denials of 
Service Requests by National Grid USA, The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National 
Grid NY and KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid, Contingency and 
Implementation Plan (filed October 21, 2019), at 4 (describing increased discounts to non-firm 
customers as necessary to attract more customers to non-firm rates). 
2  The City RDP had other important recommendations to make non-firm service more attractive, 
including elimination of the unnecessary, and costly, second mandatory test in January (Tr. 
5561-5562); developing energy efficiency programs tailored to non-firm customers (Tr. 5554-
5557); explicitly setting forth the designated interruption temperatures in the Tariff (Tr. 5554); 
modifying the Tariff to make it clear that on-site alternate fuel is not a prerequisite to taking 
non-firm service (Tr. 5559) and the reduction of penalties under certain unique circumstances 
(Tr. 5562-5565).  These too should be adopted. 
 


--- Page 7 ---
April 6, 2020 
Page 7 
 
 
A. 
If the Commission approves a lower revenue increase for the Companies, 
then we recommend that the service class increases shown in Table 2 
(KEDNY) and Table 3 (KEDLI) be scaled back proportionately, with the 
exception of the KEDLI TC/IT class, which should remain at zero increase.   
(Tr. 5546-5549.)  
 
Similarly, with respect to the National Grid’s proposal to charge non-firm customers for 
the energy efficiency transition implementation plan (“ETIP”), the City RDP explained that, if and 
when these charges are included in non-firm rates, the aforementioned rates should be adjusted so 
that the designated discounts off firm rates are maintained: 
 
Q. 
HOW SHOULD THE SBC BE APPLIED TO THE TIER 1 AND TIER 2 
RATES OF THE NON-FIRM CUSTOMERS, ASSUMING THE 
COMPANIES OFFER SPECIFIC ENERGY EFFICIENCY PROGRAMS 
FOR SUCH CUSTOMERS? 
 
A. 
The total Tier 1 and Tier 2 volumetric rates should not increase over the 
currently proposed Tier 1 and 2 rates because these rates are already greatly 
in excess of cost-based rates for Non-firm customers.   This means that the 
if the SBC is added to these customers’ rates, there should be a 
corresponding reduction in the applicable volumetric rates.  (Tr. 5556-
5557.) 
 
These adjustments are necessary to maintain the value of the proposed non-firm discounts which, 
as noted earlier, based on the ECOSS should be more robust and already may not achieve the 
desired migration to non-firm service. 
 
Finally, the DPS Staff Rate Panel (“SRP”) recommended that non-firm customers be 
charged the “incremental cost of gas,” or ICOG, rather than the weighted average cost of gas, or 
WACOG, which is what they are charged now.  (Tr. 2966.)  On rebuttal, National Grid’s Rate 
Design Panel demonstrated why such a radical change is not justified and, in fact, would be 
counter-productive and harmful to existing non-firm customers and efforts to balance supply and 
demand on the KEDNY system: 
 
Q. 
DO 
THE 
COMPANIES 
AGREE 
WITH 
THE 
SRP’S 
RECOMMENDATION TO USE ICOG INSTEAD OF WACOG IN THE 
GAS COST CALCULATION FOR NON-FIRM CUSTOMERS? 
 
A. 
No.  The Companies do not agree with this recommendation.  KEDNY and 
KEDLI’s combined portfolios include having gas available for TC and IT 
sales customers down to 15 degrees Fahrenheit.  The Companies manage 
the KEDNY and KEDLI supply portfolios on a combined basis per Case 


--- Page 8 ---
April 6, 2020 
Page 8 
 
 
05-G-0903 to maximize supply and demand synergies.  Therefore, it would 
be inequitable for any sales customers to pay only a portion of the supply 
portfolio costs.  TC and IT gas costs have been calculated using the 
WACOG since January 1, 2017.  The SRP states that the Companies have 
not created adequate incentives to prevent non-firm customer from 
migrating to firm service.  However, this recommendation would 
increase gas costs to non-firm sales customers because the ICOG is 
almost always higher than the WACOG.  In addition, the SRP’s 
argument that firm customers are subsidizing non-firm is not correct, 
because the times when the ICOG is likely to be set (e.g., the coldest 
days) are the times when non-firm customers can expect to be 
interrupted.  When this happens, non-firm customers have no cost 
responsibility for setting the ICOG.  The main result of using ICOG 
instead of WACOG would be to charge non-firm customers the costs 
they are trying to avoid, reducing their incentive to take non-firm 
service. (Tr. 2834 [emphasis added]). 
 
The fact that the Companies plan their gas procurement to include non-firm customers 
down to the designated gas interruption temperatures means that they should be treated the same 
as firm sales customers, which is exactly what the Companies are proposing by charging them 
WACOG and including them in the annual gas cost reconciliation.  (See Tr. 2835.)  Staff’s proposal 
to dramatically change how non-firm customers are charged for the cost of gas should be rejected 
because it will drive customers away from non-firm service and further strain National Grid’s 
ability to meet future demands, a result that no party wants. 
  
Depreciation 
 
The Commission should reject National Grid’s proposal to significantly increase 
depreciation rates and expense for the LPP plant accounts by shortening the recovery period for 
the remaining net book value to 20 years and by significantly increasing the estimated cost of 
removing LPP assets. Initially, as City expert witness Lane Kollen testified, National Grid’s 
proposal to shorten the amortization periods for the LPP assets is not supportable and will impose 
large and unnecessary rate increases on its customers:  
 
The Companies’ proposal to shorten the amortization period is 
unreasonable.  Once the net book value is reclassified to a regulatory asset, 
the remaining lives of the plant assets are irrelevant.  There is no compelling 
reason for the Commission to accelerate the recovery of these costs even if 
it agrees with the Companies’ plan to accelerate the retirement and 
replacement of the LPP plant assets.  The Companies’ proposal will 
compound the costs to customers, who will be required to pay for the cost 


--- Page 9 ---
April 6, 2020 
Page 9 
 
 
of the retired LPP assets as well as the cost of the replacement assets.  (Tr. 
5398.) 
 
Given the large increases sought by the Companies in these proceedings, the proposed changes to 
the LPP asset amortization periods should be rejected and the current depreciation rates 
maintained. In the alternative, Staff’s proposed 30 year amortization periods should be adopted. 
 
Similarly, the impact of the Companies’ proposed changes in calculating the cost of LPP 
removal are extreme and so unreasonable that they should not be relied on. For example, the costs 
of LPP asset removal resulting from National Grid’s flawed analyses sum to almost $900 million 
over the proposed twenty-year amortization period, nearly six times the remaining net book value 
of the plant being retired.  (Tr. 5397-5398.)  These analyses were based on a three year average of 
removal costs that was both limited and highly variable, and National Grid failed to conduct a 
reasonableness check against these statistical estimates based on their construction forecasts for 
the costs of the LPP plant replacement.  (Tr. 5405-5406.)  City witness Mr. Kollen testified that 
the Commission should reject National Grid’s effort to inflate the estimated future cost of removal 
for LPP assets because it imposes an unnecessary and wholly avoidable burden on ratepayers: 
 
The better approach is for the Commission to direct the Companies to defer 
the actual costs of removal to the regulatory assets when the costs are 
incurred and then depreciate (amortize) the increase in the regulatory assets 
using the presently approved depreciation (amortization) rates.  This 
approach is equitable to both the Companies and their customers and is fully 
compensatory to the Companies.  It will permit the Companies to recover 
100% of their actual costs of removal after they are confirmed.  (Tr. 5399.) 
 
The City’s position of maintaining the current depreciation rates and deferring the actual 
costs of removal as they are incurred for recovery using approved amortization rates should be 
adopted. This approach is equitable because it is fully compensatory to the Companies without 
imposing large and unjustifiable rate increases on customers during these extraordinary times.  
Importantly, the City’s depreciation expense adjustments would reduce the revenue requirements 
of KEDNY and KEDLI by $27.5 million and $24.5 million, respectively.  (Tr. 5395.) 
 
Respectfully submitted,

The SC-1 low income bill impacts are using the Energy Affordability Credit of a $3.00 for a customer in the Tier 1 benefit level.  
In accordance with the Order in Case 14-M-0565 issued February 17, 2017, there are six Energy Affordability Credit tiers.  
The Heating customers Tiers : Tier 1: $18.00, Tier 2: $37.00, Tier 3: $56.00, Tier 4: $42.00, Tier 5: $18.00, Tier 6: $18.00
The Non-Heating customers Tiers : Tier 1-Tier 6: $3.00

In accordance with the Order in Case 14-M-0565 issued February 17, 2017, there are six Energy Affordability Credit tiers.
